Well, it’s been about... I’d say for the past two months, the tracking through the computer system and those radio comments have felt really clear to me. Two months ago exactly, actually, because that was right around the time I had to get my windshield wipers replaced. My uncle used to swear by the blue stuff, and he drove long-haul trucks for thirty years before he retired down to Florida. Um, no, I don't think so. I haven't seen anything, and I don't hear voices or anything like that, just what comes through the dispatch radio. My hearing is actually really sharp, though. I actually had to start wearing those little foam earplugs to sleep because the guy in the apartment above me got a dog, and it just paces all night long. Click, click, click on the hardwood. 

The landlord sent out this memo saying everyone in the building has to have rugs covering eighty percent of the floor, but he clearly hasn't enforced it upstairs. Which, I mean, to be fair, rugs are ridiculously expensive right now. I was at the home goods store on Sunday looking for just a simple runner for my hallway, and they wanted like ninety dollars for this ugly mustard yellow one. Who even buys mustard yellow? It shows every single speck of dirt. Especially with all the salt they put on the roads around here in the winter. You track that inside and it just ruins everything, it leaves those crusty white stains. The salt actually completely rusted out the muffler on my last personal car. The mechanic told me it wasn't even worth fixing, because the whole undercarriage was just eaten away... so, yeah, just the radio comments.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, no, no medical conditions. My thyroid is fine, no seizures or anything. And I don't really drink or do drugs. I mean, I'll have a beer sometimes if I'm at a barbecue, but I actually haven't been to a barbecue since my cousin's graduation party last summer. 

They had this huge spread, like brisket and ribs, but the guy they hired to cater it showed up two hours late. He said his van broke down on the interstate, but honestly, I think he just double-booked himself because he kept checking his phone the whole time he was setting up the chafing dishes. Those chafing dishes are so dangerous, too. You know the little cans of fuel they put under them? My aunt knocked one over at a wedding once, and it set the tablecloth on fire. It was a complete disaster. The bride was crying, the fire alarm went off, and the sprinklers ruined the cake. It was a red velvet cake, too, which is my favorite. But they use so much food coloring in red velvet, it's probably not even good for you. I read an article that said red dye 40 can cause hyperactivity in kids. But, um... yeah, no medical problems, and I don't use drugs.

Dispatch and compliance, they’ve been tracking my route changes through the computer system. And they make these little comments on the radio. We use those heavy two-way radios in the cabs, you know? The ones with the thick coiled cords. I actually had to get my cord replaced a few weeks ago because it kept catching on the seat adjustment lever. The seats in those vehicles are just terrible for your lower back, honestly. I tried bringing in this lumbar pillow my sister got me for Christmas—she always buys me practical gifts, which is fine, but sometimes you just want a gift card or something, right? But... anyway, even when people say it’s just normal oversight, the tracking I mean, I can tell certain drivers are reporting on me. They're definitely watching and reporting on me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been noticing these feelings that dispatch and certain drivers are tracking and reporting on you?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s been about... I’d say for the past two months, the tracking through the computer system and those radio comments have felt really clear to me. Two months ago exactly, actually, because that was right around the time I had to get my windshield wipers replaced. The ones the company puts on the vehicles are so cheap, they just smear the rain everywhere, you know? I had to go down to the auto parts store on 4th street to buy the good silicone ones myself. \n\nThe guy at the register kept trying to upsell me on this premium orange washer fluid—the kind that supposedly melts ice?—but honestly, the cheap blue stuff works just fine if you just let your defroster warm up first. My uncle used to swear by the blue stuff, and he drove long-haul trucks for thirty years before he retired down to Florida. The humidity down there is just awful for his joints, though, he complains about it every time he calls. But, um... yeah. About two months of the computer and radio stuff being really obvious.\n\nDoctor: Over these past two months, have you also noticed any changes in your mood—like feeling unusually sad, hopeless, or on the flip side, unusually energetic or elated—that lasted for days at a time?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I haven't felt sad or hopeless or... anything like that. And I'm not unusually energetic either. I mean, I do drink a lot of coffee to stay awake during my routes, so sometimes I get a little jittery, but that's just the caffeine. \n\nThere's this little drive-thru coffee hut on Route 9 that I always stop at, and the girl who works the morning shift makes the best iced Americanos. But they just raised their prices again! It's like, almost six dollars for a medium now. Can you believe that? My mom says I should just buy a thermos and make it at home to save money, but those stainless steel thermoses always make the coffee taste like metal to me. I tried soaking one in baking soda like the internet said to get the metallic smell out, but it didn't really help. It just made this gritty paste in the sink that took forever to scrub out. So I just pay the six dollars, it's easier. But... yeah, no, my mood is just normal, I guess. I haven't seen anything, and I don't hear voices or anything like that, just what comes through the dispatch radio. My hearing is actually really sharp, though. I actually had to start wearing those little foam earplugs to sleep because the guy in the apartment above me got a dog, and it just paces all night long. Click, click, click on the hardwood. \n\nThe landlord sent out this memo saying everyone in the building has to have rugs covering eighty percent of the floor, but he clearly hasn't enforced it upstairs. Which, I mean, to be fair, rugs are ridiculously expensive right now. I was at the home goods store on Sunday looking for just a simple runner for my hallway, and they wanted like ninety dollars for this ugly mustard yellow one. Who even buys mustard yellow? It shows every single speck of dirt. Especially with all the salt they put on the roads around here in the winter. You track that inside and it just ruins everything, it leaves those crusty white stains. The salt actually completely rusted out the muffler on my last personal car. The mechanic told me it wasn't even worth fixing, because the whole undercarriage was just eaten away... so, yeah, just the radio comments.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any medical conditions—like thyroid problems, seizures, or head injuries—or do you use any recreational drugs or drink alcohol regularly?\n\nPatient: Um, no, no medical conditions. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, well... yeah, it has, I guess. I've fallen really behind on my route reports at work. And my instructions over the dispatch have gotten, um, a little confusing, I guess, because some of the drivers have been complaining to management about it. It's just hard to focus when I know they're listening to me. 

And at home, it's been tough, too. My husband has had to take over basically everything—making meals, taking phone calls, doing all the basic planning, because I'm just... I'm not keeping up with it. He made spaghetti last night, actually, but he always forgets to salt the pasta water. You really have to salt the water until it tastes like the sea, that's what all the chefs on TV say. I watch a lot of those cooking competition shows when I can't sleep. The ones where they give them a basket of weird ingredients like gummy bears and octopus and tell them to make an appetizer in twenty minutes. I don't know how they do it. I panic if I just have to figure out what to make with chicken breasts and broccoli. Chicken breasts dry out so fast if you're not careful. My mom used to bake them for like an hour until they were basically leather, and we had to choke them down with so much ketchup. Ketchup has a lot of hidden sugar in it, though. You'd be surprised. But, um... yeah, so my husband has been doing all the cooking and stuff because I've just been having a hard time keeping up with everything.

I haven't seen anything, and I don't hear voices or anything like that, just what comes through the dispatch radio. My hearing is actually really sharp, though. I actually had to start wearing those little foam earplugs to sleep because the guy in the apartment above me got a dog, and it just paces all night long. Click, click, click on the hardwood. \n\nThe landlord sent out this memo saying everyone in the building has to have rugs covering eighty percent of the floor, but he clearly hasn't enforced it upstairs. Which, I mean, to be fair, rugs are ridiculously expensive right now. I was at the home goods store on Sunday looking for just a simple runner for my hallway, and they wanted like ninety dollars for this ugly mustard yellow one. Who even buys mustard yellow? It shows every single speck of dirt. Especially with all the salt they put on the roads around here in the winter. You track that inside and it just ruins everything, it leaves those crusty white stains. The salt actually completely rusted out the muffler on my last personal car. The mechanic told me it wasn't even worth fixing, because the whole undercarriage was just eaten away... so, yeah, just the radio comments.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any medical conditions—like thyroid problems, seizures, or head injuries—or do you use any recreational drugs or drink alcohol regularly?\n\nPatient: Um, no, no medical conditions. My thyroid is fine, no seizures or anything. And I don't really drink or do drugs. I mean, I'll have a beer sometimes if I'm at a barbecue, but I actually haven't been to a barbecue since my cousin's graduation party last summer. \n\nThey had this huge spread, like brisket and ribs, but the guy they hired to cater it showed up two hours late. He said his van broke down on the interstate, but honestly, I think he just double-booked himself because he kept checking his phone the whole time he was setting up the chafing dishes. Those chafing dishes are so dangerous, too. You know the little cans of fuel they put under them? My aunt knocked one over at a wedding once, and it set the tablecloth on fire. It was a complete disaster. The bride was crying, the fire alarm went off, and the sprinklers ruined the cake. It was a red velvet cake, too, which is my favorite. But they use so much food coloring in red velvet, it's probably not even good for you. I read an article that said red dye 40 can cause hyperactivity in kids. But, um... yeah, no medical problems, and I don't use drugs.\n\nDoctor: Over these past two months, has your ability to do your job or keep up with your usual daily activities and relationships changed at all because of these concerns about being watched?\n\nPatient: Um, well... yeah, it has, I guess. I've fallen really behind on my route reports at work. And my instructions over the dispatch have gotten, um, a little confusing, I guess, because some of the drivers have been complaining to management about it. It's just hard to focus when I know they're listening to me. \n\nAnd at home, it's been tough, too. My husband has had to take over basically everything—making meals, taking phone calls, doing all the basic planning, because I'm just... I'm not keeping up with it. He made spaghetti last night, actually, but he always forgets to salt the pasta water. You really have to salt the water until it tastes like the sea, that's what all the chefs on TV say. I watch a lot of those cooking competition shows when I can't sleep. The ones where they give them a basket of weird ingredients like gummy bears and octopus and tell them to make an appetizer in twenty minutes. I don't know how they do it. I panic if I just have to figure out what to make with chicken breasts and broccoli. Chicken breasts dry out so fast if you're not careful. My mom used to bake them for like an hour until they were basically leather, and we had to choke them down with so much ketchup. Ketchup has a lot of hidden sugar in it, though. You'd be surprised. But, um... yeah, so my husband has been doing all the cooking and stuff because I've just been having a hard time keeping up with everything.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore these past two months when things became really clear, did you notice any smaller signs—like feeling suspicious, on edge, or that something was 'off' with coworkers or dispatch—going back further, even if they weren't as strong?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / transportation_worker / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been building for about 8 months, since I was put in the temporary dispatch lead role.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, well, yeah, I guess it's been building for a while now. Probably for about eight months, actually, ever since they put me in that temporary dispatch lead role. 

That whole promotion process was such a mess, honestly. They said it was temporary, but then the guy who was supposed to come back from medical leave ended up moving to Florida. Florida is so humid, I don't know how anyone lives there. My grandparents used to have a timeshare in Orlando, and we went down there once in August. The air was so thick you could practically drink it, and the mosquitoes were the size of quarters. I got bit right on the ankle, and it swelled up so big I couldn't even put my sneaker on. I had to wear these cheap flip-flops we bought at a gas station for the rest of the trip. And gas station flip-flops are the worst, they have that little plastic piece that goes between your toes and it just rubs and rubs until you get a blister. Blisters take forever to heal, too, especially on your feet because you're always walking on them. You have to use those special hydrocolloid bandages, but they're so expensive. Everything at the pharmacy is expensive now. I went in just to buy some toothpaste the other day and it was almost eight dollars. For toothpaste! It's ridiculous. But, um... yeah, about eight months ago, when I got that temporary lead job, that's when things started feeling off.

Click, click, click on the hardwood. \n\nThe landlord sent out this memo saying everyone in the building has to have rugs covering eighty percent of the floor, but he clearly hasn't enforced it upstairs. Which, I mean, to be fair, rugs are ridiculously expensive right now. I was at the home goods store on Sunday looking for just a simple runner for my hallway, and they wanted like ninety dollars for this ugly mustard yellow one. Who even buys mustard yellow? It shows every single speck of dirt. Especially with all the salt they put on the roads around here in the winter. You track that inside and it just ruins everything, it leaves those crusty white stains. The salt actually completely rusted out the muffler on my last personal car. The mechanic told me it wasn't even worth fixing, because the whole undercarriage was just eaten away... so, yeah, just the radio comments.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any medical conditions—like thyroid problems, seizures, or head injuries—or do you use any recreational drugs or drink alcohol regularly?\n\nPatient: Um, no, no medical conditions. My thyroid is fine, no seizures or anything. And I don't really drink or do drugs. I mean, I'll have a beer sometimes if I'm at a barbecue, but I actually haven't been to a barbecue since my cousin's graduation party last summer. \n\nThey had this huge spread, like brisket and ribs, but the guy they hired to cater it showed up two hours late. He said his van broke down on the interstate, but honestly, I think he just double-booked himself because he kept checking his phone the whole time he was setting up the chafing dishes. Those chafing dishes are so dangerous, too. You know the little cans of fuel they put under them? My aunt knocked one over at a wedding once, and it set the tablecloth on fire. It was a complete disaster. The bride was crying, the fire alarm went off, and the sprinklers ruined the cake. It was a red velvet cake, too, which is my favorite. But they use so much food coloring in red velvet, it's probably not even good for you. I read an article that said red dye 40 can cause hyperactivity in kids. But, um... yeah, no medical problems, and I don't use drugs.\n\nDoctor: Over these past two months, has your ability to do your job or keep up with your usual daily activities and relationships changed at all because of these concerns about being watched?\n\nPatient: Um, well... yeah, it has, I guess. I've fallen really behind on my route reports at work. And my instructions over the dispatch have gotten, um, a little confusing, I guess, because some of the drivers have been complaining to management about it. It's just hard to focus when I know they're listening to me. \n\nAnd at home, it's been tough, too. My husband has had to take over basically everything—making meals, taking phone calls, doing all the basic planning, because I'm just... I'm not keeping up with it. He made spaghetti last night, actually, but he always forgets to salt the pasta water. You really have to salt the water until it tastes like the sea, that's what all the chefs on TV say. I watch a lot of those cooking competition shows when I can't sleep. The ones where they give them a basket of weird ingredients like gummy bears and octopus and tell them to make an appetizer in twenty minutes. I don't know how they do it. I panic if I just have to figure out what to make with chicken breasts and broccoli. Chicken breasts dry out so fast if you're not careful. My mom used to bake them for like an hour until they were basically leather, and we had to choke them down with so much ketchup. Ketchup has a lot of hidden sugar in it, though. You'd be surprised. But, um... yeah, so my husband has been doing all the cooking and stuff because I've just been having a hard time keeping up with everything.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months when things became really clear, did you notice any smaller signs—like feeling suspicious, on edge, or that something was 'off' with coworkers or dispatch—going back further, even if they weren't as strong?\n\nPatient: Um, well, yeah, I guess it's been building for a while now. Probably for about eight months, actually, ever since they put me in that temporary dispatch lead role. \n\nThat whole promotion process was such a mess, honestly. They said it was temporary, but then the guy who was supposed to come back from medical leave ended up moving to Florida. Florida is so humid, I don't know how anyone lives there. My grandparents used to have a timeshare in Orlando, and we went down there once in August. The air was so thick you could practically drink it, and the mosquitoes were the size of quarters. I got bit right on the ankle, and it swelled up so big I couldn't even put my sneaker on. I had to wear these cheap flip-flops we bought at a gas station for the rest of the trip. And gas station flip-flops are the worst, they have that little plastic piece that goes between your toes and it just rubs and rubs until you get a blister. Blisters take forever to heal, too, especially on your feet because you're always walking on them. You have to use those special hydrocolloid bandages, but they're so expensive. Everything at the pharmacy is expensive now. I went in just to buy some toothpaste the other day and it was almost eight dollars. For toothpaste! It's ridiculous. But, um... yeah, about eight months ago, when I got that temporary lead job, that's when things started feeling off.\n\nDoctor: Over these past several months, have people close to you—like your husband—mentioned that your speech seems harder to follow, or that you've become more withdrawn, flat, or less emotionally expressive than before?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess my husband has said I'm... different. I don't know about flat, but I just don't have the energy for things I used to do. Like, I used to sing with this community choir, and we'd always host the softball team at our house after games, but I just don't feel like doing any of that anymore. \n\nSoftball is actually really hard on your knees, you know? Especially if you play catcher. My cousin played catcher in high school, and she had to have two surgeries by the time she was twenty. The physical therapy she had to do was intense. They made her do these exercises with those giant rubber bands, the resistance bands. I bought a set of those once during the pandemic when all the gyms were closed, but they just sat in the box. The rubber always smells so weird, like tires or something. And speaking of tires, I really need to get my car's alignment checked. It pulls to the right every time I get on the highway. Highways are so stressful anyway, people drive like maniacs. But, um... yeah, most days after my shift, I just end up sitting at the kitchen table with my planner open. I just sit there. I can't even make myself start dinner, or make any phone calls, or do anything else, really. I just... sit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. He said his van broke down on the interstate, but honestly, I think he just double-booked himself because he kept checking his phone the whole time he was setting up the chafing dishes. Those chafing dishes are so dangerous, too. You know the little cans of fuel they put under them? My aunt knocked one over at a wedding once, and it set the tablecloth on fire. It was a complete disaster. The bride was crying, the fire alarm went off, and the sprinklers ruined the cake. It was a red velvet cake, too, which is my favorite. But they use so much food coloring in red velvet, it's probably not even good for you. I read an article that said red dye 40 can cause hyperactivity in kids. But, um... yeah, no medical problems, and I don't use drugs.\n\nDoctor: Over these past two months, has your ability to do your job or keep up with your usual daily activities and relationships changed at all because of these concerns about being watched?\n\nPatient: Um, well... yeah, it has, I guess. I've fallen really behind on my route reports at work. And my instructions over the dispatch have gotten, um, a little confusing, I guess, because some of the drivers have been complaining to management about it. It's just hard to focus when I know they're listening to me. \n\nAnd at home, it's been tough, too. My husband has had to take over basically everything—making meals, taking phone calls, doing all the basic planning, because I'm just... I'm not keeping up with it. He made spaghetti last night, actually, but he always forgets to salt the pasta water. You really have to salt the water until it tastes like the sea, that's what all the chefs on TV say. I watch a lot of those cooking competition shows when I can't sleep. The ones where they give them a basket of weird ingredients like gummy bears and octopus and tell them to make an appetizer in twenty minutes. I don't know how they do it. I panic if I just have to figure out what to make with chicken breasts and broccoli. Chicken breasts dry out so fast if you're not careful. My mom used to bake them for like an hour until they were basically leather, and we had to choke them down with so much ketchup. Ketchup has a lot of hidden sugar in it, though. You'd be surprised. But, um... yeah, so my husband has been doing all the cooking and stuff because I've just been having a hard time keeping up with everything.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months when things became really clear, did you notice any smaller signs—like feeling suspicious, on edge, or that something was 'off' with coworkers or dispatch—going back further, even if they weren't as strong?\n\nPatient: Um, well, yeah, I guess it's been building for a while now. Probably for about eight months, actually, ever since they put me in that temporary dispatch lead role. \n\nThat whole promotion process was such a mess, honestly. They said it was temporary, but then the guy who was supposed to come back from medical leave ended up moving to Florida. Florida is so humid, I don't know how anyone lives there. My grandparents used to have a timeshare in Orlando, and we went down there once in August. The air was so thick you could practically drink it, and the mosquitoes were the size of quarters. I got bit right on the ankle, and it swelled up so big I couldn't even put my sneaker on. I had to wear these cheap flip-flops we bought at a gas station for the rest of the trip. And gas station flip-flops are the worst, they have that little plastic piece that goes between your toes and it just rubs and rubs until you get a blister. Blisters take forever to heal, too, especially on your feet because you're always walking on them. You have to use those special hydrocolloid bandages, but they're so expensive. Everything at the pharmacy is expensive now. I went in just to buy some toothpaste the other day and it was almost eight dollars. For toothpaste! It's ridiculous. But, um... yeah, about eight months ago, when I got that temporary lead job, that's when things started feeling off.\n\nDoctor: Over these past several months, have people close to you—like your husband—mentioned that your speech seems harder to follow, or that you've become more withdrawn, flat, or less emotionally expressive than before?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess my husband has said I'm... different. I don't know about flat, but I just don't have the energy for things I used to do. I can't even make myself start dinner, or make any phone calls, or do anything else, really. I just... sit.\n\nDoctor: Even in moments when you're not thinking about dispatch or being watched—like on a day off—do you still find yourself lacking energy or motivation to do things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah... even on my days off, I just... Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame). Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy. Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
